**Q.** WHAT ARE THE RESULTS OF THE RESIDENTIAL CUSTOMER COST ANALYSIS YOU CONDUCTED FOR THIS CASE?

**A.** My Schedule GAW-7 provides the details of my Residential Rate RS customer cost analysis, which is conducted using both the Company’s requested 11.50% ROE as well as OCA’s recommended ROE of 9.10%. As indicated in this Schedule, my analysis produces a Residential customer cost range of $11.52 to $12.10 per month.

**Q.** WHAT IS YOUR RECOMMENDATION CONCERNING DUQUESNE’S RESIDENTIAL CUSTOMER CHARGES?

**A.** Although my customer cost analysis (that includes a provision for overhead) indicates that a customer charge of no more than about $12.10 is warranted, I recommend that the current Residential customer charge of $12.50 be maintained. This maintaining of the current customer charge will promote rate continuity as well as promoting conservation as any increase authorized in this case will be collected from the Residential energy charges thereby, sending a more appropriate price signal for customers to conserve and use energy more efficiently.

### V. RESIDENTIAL EV TIME-OF-USE PILOT

**Q.** PLEASE PROVIDE A GENERAL OVERVIEW OF THE COMPANY’S PROPOSED RESIDENTIAL ELECTRIC VEHICLE TIME-OF-USE (“TOU”) PILOT PROGRAM.

**A.** Duquesne is proposing an optional (opt in) Residential TOU pilot rate program available to owners of electric vehicles based on: (1) each customer’s current

<!-- PDF page 6101 (printed p. 44) -->

rate schedule; and, (2) whether the customer elects to have a whole house TOU rate or whether they elect a rate that is specific to their EV charger. In all, the Company proposes six different TOU rates available only to Residential customers with electric vehicles as shown below:

- Residential Standard: Whole Home; EV Charger Only
- Residential Heating: Whole Home; EV Charger Only
- Residential Add-On Heat Pump: Whole Home; EV Charger Only

> Extractor note (not source text): the PDF prints these six rate options as a table-like list (three rate schedules, each with a Whole Home and an EV Charger Only option); rendered here as a list.

<!-- PDF page 6103 (printed p. 46) -->

**Q.** DO YOU SUPPORT THE COMPANY’S PROPOSAL TO OFFER SIX DIFFERENT RESIDENTIAL TOU RATES AVAILABLE TO EV OWNERS?

**A.** No. OCA and I object to the Company’s proposed whole home TOU rates available only to EV owners. In my view, the Company’s proposed EV whole home TOU rate is discriminatory against those customers without an electric vehicle. Perhaps more importantly is that there should not be new whole home rates offered based simply on the inventory of specific equipment and appliances within an individual customer’s home. With this being said, OCA and I do not object to an ancillary rate specific to EV chargers; i.e., a separately sub-metered[^wat23] and dedicated EV charger TOU rate wherein the remainder of the home is charged at the standard non-TOU rate.

**Q.** IN TABLE 15 ABOVE, IT IS OBSERVED THAT THE COMPANY’S PROPOSED ON-PEAK RATES ARE THE SAME AS THE CORRESPONDING NON-TIME-DIFFERENTIATED RATE FOR EACH RATE SCHEDULE AND THE OFF-PEAK RATES ARE PRICED LOWER THAN THE ON-PEAK RATE. WILL THIS RESULT IN A SUBSIDIZED RATE TO CUSTOMERS THAT ELECT A TOU RATE?

**A.** Yes. In developing its proposed EV TOU rates, the Company simply set the on-peak rate (for each of the three basic Residential rates – RS, RH, and RA) at the standard non-time-differentiated rate and then discounted the rate for the off-peak period. It must be remembered that the standard rates are not time-differentiated such that they reflect the energy usage (and loads) throughout the year – both on-peak and off-peak. By mathematical definition, the Company’s proposed EV rates are nothing more than subsidized promotional rates. In case this is not clear, the standard (non-TOU) rate is a blend of both higher cost on-peak usage and lower cost off-peak usage.

[^wat23]: I use the term sub-meter here generically as the Company’s proposed EV only TOU pilot does not have the Company actually installing a meter but instead, they propose to hire a third party contractor to use a technology based internet communication device to transmit usage. This is the same technology that will be used in the “managed charging” pilot that is discussed by OCA witness DeMarco. While Duquesne is proposing to install some sub-meters in 50 locations, it is my understand that they doing to simply to confirm validity of the Wi-Fi transmitted data.

<!-- PDF page 6104 (printed p. 47) -->

This blended rate then serves as the TOU on-peak rate and then the TOU off-peak rate is discounted from there.

**A.** Yes. Based on the Company’s forecasted usages throughout the year (summer and winter) as well as the forecasted EV on-peak and off-peak usage levels provided in response to OCA-V-12, I have calculated the weighted average annual and seasonal EV discounts under the Company’s proposed rate design as shown in the tables below:

| RA (Non-TOU)                           |                       $0.0356 |                            -- |                             -- |
| RA TOU Whole Home (On-Peak + Off-Peak) |                       $0.0312 |                      -$0.0044 |                          12.4% |
| RA TOU Only (On-Peak + Off-Peak)       |                       $0.0178 |                      -$0.0178 |                          50.1% |

<!-- PDF page 6106 (printed p. 49) -->

During the winter months, those EV owners that elect the whole home TOU rate would enjoy discounts from non-EV owners ranging from 11.0% (Rate RH) to 16.9% (Rate RS). For those EV owners that elect the EV charger only rate, these customers would enjoy discounts of 50.1% from non-EV owners.

**Q.** IN THE COMPANY’S OPINION, ARE THE PROPOSED TOU EV RATES PROMOTIONAL OR SUBSIDIZED RATES?

**A.** No. In response to OCA-V-30 and OCA-V-31, the Company claimed that its proposed TOU EV rate design does not result in subsidized rates based on its opinion that the incremental usage and load during off-peak hours would not result in higher allocated distribution system costs.

While it is true that both incremental and total off-peak usage and load during off-peak hours is less costly to Duquesne than on-peak usage, the opposite is true for incremental or total usage during on-peak hours; i.e., on-peak usage is more costly than off-peak usage. Duquesne ignores this important distinction in that the Company’s proposed on-peak rate is the same as the blended non-time-differentiated rate (that includes both high cost on-peak hours and low cost off-peak hours). As an analogy, and taking Duquesne’s philosophy to its logical conclusion, one could then argue that the incremental off-peak load for new customers does not increase the Company’s system costs such that all new customers should be afforded a discounted TOU rate wherein the on-peak rate is priced at the same rate as legacy (current customers) and the off-peak rate should be significantly discounted from the on-peak rate.

**Q.** DO YOU SUPPORT THE COMPANY’S PROPOSED RESIDENTIAL SUBSIDIZED TOU RATES THAT WOULD BE AVAILABLE ONLY TO EV OWNERS?

**A.** No. In the event the Commission authorizes some form of TOU rates available to Residential EV owners, the resulting rates should be revenue neutral and not simply a promotional rate that must be subsidized by non-EV owners. I will discuss my recommended EV TOU rate design later in my testimony.

<!-- PDF page 6107 (printed p. 50) -->

**Q.** IN EVALUATING THE COMPANY’S PROPOSED EV CHARGER ONLY TOU RATE DESIGN, DID YOU OBSERVE AN UNREALISTIC ASSUMPTION REGARDING THE LEVEL OF USAGE DURING ON-PEAK AND OFF-PEAK HOURS?

**A.** Yes. In response to OCA-V-12, the Company provided its proposed on-peak and off-peak EV charger only billing determinants. As shown in the table below, the Company forecasts that 97% of EV charger only energy usage will occur during off-peak hours:

**TABLE 19 — Duquesne Forecasted On-Peak & Off-Peak KWH: EV Charger Only**

| Class | On-Peak | Off-Peak | % Off-Peak |
| ----- | ------: | -------: | ---------: |
| RS    |   6,318 |  200,797 |      96.9% |
| RH    |     189 |    6,026 |      97.0% |
| RA    |      91 |    2,893 |      97.0% |

This is an unrealistic assumption. To illustrate, there will be approximately 1,535 on-peak hours of the year wherein off-peak hours will represent about 82% of the total hours in the year.[^wat24] Furthermore, in response to OCA-V-5, the Company provided Residential loads for every hour of the year during 2022. Based on the Company’s definition of on-peak and off-peak hours, Residential energy consumption was 78.8% during off-peak hours and 21.2% during on-peak hours. Finally, Duquesne witness Olexsak states in her direct testimony that Potomac Edison in Maryland has an EV only TOU rate and approximately 80% of charging occurred during off-peak hours.[^wat25]

Considering that the Company’s proposed on-peak period is from 3:00 p.m. to 9:00 p.m. weekdays, it is hard to imagine that only 3% of charger usage will occur during the on-peak period. This is because most EV owners will charge their vehicles when returning home from work in the evening and while some may elect to not

[^wat24]: 6 hours on-peak times 5 days a week times 52 weeks = 1,560 less about 4 holiday hours during weekdays = 1,535. 1,535 divided by 8,760 total hours = 17.52% on-peak hours.

[^wat25]: Direct Testimony of Olexsak, page 59.

<!-- PDF page 6108 (printed p. 51) -->

charge their vehicle until after 9:00 p.m., it is likely that many will begin charging their vehicles before the end of the on-peak period.[^wat26]

**Q.** DID THE COMPANY PROVIDE ANY BASIS FOR ITS ASSUMPTION THAT 97% OF EV CHARGER USAGE WILL OCCUR DURING OFF-PEAK PERIODS?

**A.** Not that I can find. I have reviewed the testimony, exhibits, and data provided by witnesses Olexsak, Coleman, Ogden, and Gorman and can find no support for this assumption other than the hard-keyed amounts in the revenue proof provided in OCA-V-12.

**Q.** IN DESIGNING EV CHARGER ONLY TOU RATES, WHAT IS A MORE REASONABLE ASSUMPTION OF OFF-PEAK VERSUS ON-PEAK ENERGY USAGE?

**A.** While the exact separation between off-peak and on-peak usage is not known since this will be a new rate schedule, it is my opinion that for designing the EV charger only rate, 80% off-peak 20% on-peak is more appropriate until actual data is obtained.

**Q.** HAVE YOU DESIGNED AN ANCILLARY RESIDENTIAL EV CHARGER ONLY TOU RATE THAT IS NOT SUBSIDIZED BY OTHER CUSTOMERS AND AT THE SAME TIME PROVIDES INCENTIVES FOR THESE CUSTOMERS TO CHARGE THEIR VEHICLES DURING OFF-PEAK HOURS?

**A.** Yes. I recommend the only Residential TOU rate that should be approved in this case is an ancillary (supplemental) rate dedicated only to the EV charger which would be separately sub-metered.[^wat27] Since this would be a supplemental rate for EV chargers only, there should be a single TOU EV only charger rate approved,

[^wat26]: In addition, the load and energy used to charge an EV is larger during the early stages of the charging cycle than the latter stages.

[^wat27]: Again, I am using this term generically. The Company does not have to install a separate meter, but rather can rely on technology based internet communication device to transmit usage via Wi-Fi.

<!-- PDF page 6109 (printed p. 52) -->

regardless of whether a customer takes service under Rate RS, Rate RH, or Rate RA. Furthermore, this EV only charger rate should be revenue neutral and not subsidized by all other Residential customers.

In developing my Residential EV charger only TOU rate that is not subsidized but also provides incentives for customers to charge their EVs during cheaper off-peak hours, I relied upon the data contained in the Company’s revenue proof and rate design provided in response to OCA-V-12 (adjusted for 80% off-peak/20% on-peak usage). In summary, I calculated a TOU rate that is not subsidized, and is revenue neutral to all Residential customers. In doing so, I selected a relative on-peak to off-peak price differential (ratio of on-peak to off-peak rate).

**Q.** HOW DID YOU SELECT A RATIO OF ON-PEAK TO OFF-PEAK RATES FOR YOUR RECOMMENDED EV CHARGER ONLY ANCILLARY RATE?

**A.** This determination relating to Duquesne’s distribution service was subjective in nature. While TOU distribution only rates are not very common in the electric industry, there are numerous Residential TOU rates offered for vertically integrated electric utilities and are designed, at least to some extent, on the avoided or marginal cost of generation (capacity and energy). In determining the ratio of the on-peak rate to the off-peak rate there should be a reasonable differential between the higher cost on-peak rate and the lower cost off-peak rate but not so large as to dramatically inhibit participation.

Under the Company’s various proposed EV TOU rates, the following on-peak to off-peak ratios were developed:

<!-- PDF page 6110 (printed p. 53) -->

**TABLE 20 — Duquesne Proposed Ratio of On-Peak to Off-Peak Rates**

| Rate               | Season | Ratio |
| ------------------ | ------ | ----: |
| RS Whole Home      |        |  125% |
| RS EV Charger Only |        |  206% |
| RH Whole Home      | Summer |  125% |
| RH Whole Home      | Winter |  115% |
| RH EV Charger Only | Summer |  207% |
| RH EV Charger Only | Winter |  207% |
| RA Whole Home      | Summer |  119% |
| RA Whole Home      | Winter |  117% |
| RA EV Charger Only | Summer |  207% |
| RA EV Charger Only | Winter |  207% |

As indicated above, the on-peak to off-peak ratios recommended by Duquesne range from about 115% to a little over 200%. In my opinion, a ratio in the range of 150% to 200% for the ancillary EV charger only TOU rate is reasonable and appropriate for purposes of this pilot program.

**Q.** PLEASE PROVIDE YOUR RECOMMENDED EV CHARGER ONLY RATES FOR THIS CASE.

**A.** I have designed an ancillary TOU EV charger only rate based on the Company’s proposed Residential revenue increases, energy, and customer charges.

<!-- PDF page 6111 (printed p. 54) -->

**TABLE 21 — OCA EV Charger Only Rate (¢/KWH)**

| Rating Period | 150% On-Peak To Off-Peak Ratio | 200% On-Peak To Off-Peak Ratio |
| ------------- | -----------------------------: | -----------------------------: |
| On-Peak       |                      11.3832 ¢ |                      13.9128 ¢ |
| Off-Peak      |                       7.5888 ¢ |                       6.9564 ¢ |
| On-Peak Ratio |                           150% |                           200% |

Period Ratio Ratio

| On-Peak       | 11.3832 ¢ | 13.9128 ¢ |
| ------------- | --------: | --------: |
| Off-Peak      |  7.5888 ¢ |  6.9564 ¢ |
| On-Peak Ratio |      150% |      200% |

While the TOU rate based on a 200% on-peak to off-peak ratio provides a greater differential between on-peak and off-peak usage, the rate design using a 150% ratio is recommended as a starting point for this pilot rate. The calculations supporting my EV charger only rate design is provided in my Schedule GAW-8.

**Q.** DOES THIS COMPLETE YOUR TESTIMONY?

**A.** Yes.

## OCA Statement 4R — Rebuttal Testimony of Glenn A. Watkins (OCA) — PDF pp. 6260–6262

> Extractor note (not source text): Excerpt (verification at PDF p. 6263): response to Knecht’s alternative cost-of-service study and revenue allocation.

<!-- PDF page 6260 (printed p. 6) -->

Knecht effectively combined the URD and Radial subsystems (within the demand portion), he assigns significantly more costs to the Residential class and significantly fewer costs to the Commercial classes.

**Q.** WHAT ARE YOUR CONCLUSIONS REGARDING THE VERACITY OF MR. KNECHT’S ALTERNATIVE CCOSS?

**A.** Mr. Knecht’s alternative CCOSS is based on arbitrary and inconsistent adjustments and not based on any supportable assumptions. Therefore, the results of Mr. Knecht’s CCOSS cannot be considered credible in any way and therefore should not be given any consideration in this proceeding.

### III. REVENUE ALLOCATIONS

**Q.** CAN YOU PROVIDE A COMPARISON OF THE VARIOUS PARTIES’ RECOMMENDED CLASS REVENUE ALLOCATIONS?

**A.** Yes. Duquesne, OSBA and OCA provided their recommended class revenue allocations based on the Company’s proposed $101 million overall rate revenue increase. The following table presents Duquesne’s, OSBA’s and OCA’s revenue allocation recommendation in terms of each class’s percentage of the total dollar increase as well as each class’s percentage of the system average percentage increase:

As can be observed from the table above, the OCA’s and Duquesne’s recommendations are fairly similar, at least in relative terms. OSBA recommends significantly larger increases to each of the Residential rate schedules and Rate GS. At the same time, OSBA recommends either no increases or significantly smaller increases to the GM<25, GM>25, GMH>25, GL, and L rate schedules.

**Q.** HOW DID OSBA WITNESS KNECHT DEVELOP HIS RECOMMENDED CLASS REVENUE ALLOCATIONS?

**A.** Mr. Knecht relied primarily on the results of his alternative CCOSS but placed increase constraints of 150% of the system average increase on individual classes and placed a constraint that no class receive a rate decrease.

**Q.** WHAT ARE YOUR CONCLUSIONS REGARDING MR. KNECHT’S PROPOSED CLASS REVENUE ALLOCATIONS?

**A.** First, Mr. Knecht’s class revenue allocations are predicated upon his alternative class cost of service study in which I recommend that no weight be given to that study. Second, I observed that with regard to the Residential class, which is

<!-- PDF page 6262 (printed p. 8) -->

Duquesne’s largest class, Mr. Knecht’s recommendation is clearly an outlier wherein he proposes much higher increases than either Duquesne or OCA. At the same time, Mr. Knecht recommends no increases to several of the Commercial and Lighting classes (GM>25, GMH>25, GL and L). Mr. Knecht’s recommendations for no, or minimal, increases to most Commercial classes are the direct result of his unfounded cost shifting away from Commercial customers to Residential customers. As a result, I recommend that Mr. Knecht’s class revenue allocations not be considered or given any weight.

**Q.** DOES THIS COMPLETE YOUR REBUTTAL TESTIMONY?

**A.** Yes.

**Q.** PLEASE RESPOND TO MR. GORMAN’S FIRST ASSERTION THAT HE SIMPLY UPDATED SOME ALLOCATORS.

**A.** Mr. Gorman’s statement is not entirely accurate. That is, while a few allocators were indeed “updated” in his new version of the CCOSS, he actually uses a different set of allocators to assign various underground plant costs. Mr. Gorman’s selection of new allocators relating to underground costs have a significant impact on the costs assigned across classes as shown in the table below:

As indicated above and as will be explained in more detail later, Mr. Gorman’s use of new allocation factors results in $274.2 million more plant costs assigned to the residential class, $6.1 million less assigned to the GS class, $136.8 million less to the GM/GMH class, $92.1 million less to the GL/GLH class, $38.3 million less to the Lighting classes and $0.9 million less to the Unmetered Service (“UMS”) class. These changes in allocation factors had a profound impact on the ultimate level of costs assigned to classes and the resulting class rates of return (“RORs”), which of

<!-- PDF page 6366 (printed p. 6) -->

course resulted in many more costs being assigned to residential customers and substantially fewer costs allocated to non-residential customers.

**Q.** PLEASE IDENTIFY AND EXPLAIN MR. GORMAN’S SELECTION OF NEW ALLOCATORS TO ASSIGN UNDERGROUND PLANT COSTS.

**A.** Mr. Gorman’s use of new allocators relates to his assignment of the “Radial” underground costs. In this regard, it should be remembered that what is referred to as Radial is total underground plant costs less those costs attributable to the Downtown Network (all non-residential) and the Underground Residential Development (all residential). In his original filing (and consistent with all prior cases), Mr. Gorman allocated the “Radial” demand costs based on class contributions to non-coincident peak (“NCP”) demands based on an allocator called “NCP-Prim-Rad” (primary voltage) and a corresponding allocator called “NCP-Sec-Rad” (secondary voltage). In his new version, he allocated the “Radial” costs on an allocator called “NCP-Prim-NonNet” and “NCP-Sec-NonNet.” While Mr. Gorman’s naming conventions are somewhat confusing at first glance, the following tables provide a comparison of various primary and secondary NCP allocation amounts and resulting factors used to allocate Radial (not dedicated to DTN or URD) underground costs to classes:

To illustrate, under Mr. Gorman’s original study (as well as all previous cases), the RS class was assigned about 2% of the Radial costs. However, under his new study, the RS class is assigned about 42% to 43% of these costs.

**Q.** CAN YOU EXPLAIN WHY THERE IS SUCH A DRAMATIC CHANGE IN THE RADIAL NCP ALLOCATION FACTORS BETWEEN MR. GORMAN’S ORIGINAL AND PRIOR STUDIES TO THOSE HE USED IN HIS REBUTTAL TESTIMONY?

**A.** Yes. In Mr. Gorman’s original and prior studies, it was determined that 97.50% of residential customers do not use the underground Radial subsystem.[^watsr1] In other words, almost all underground facilities that serve residential customers are within the URD portion of total underground facilities such that only about 2.50% of residential demand is associated with the underground Radial subsystem.

[^watsr1]: Per Attachment OCA-V-10 (Allocators).xlsx, Tab: Demands, Excel row 97.

## OSBA Statement No. 1 — Direct Testimony and Exhibits of Robert D. Knecht (Office of Small Business Advocate) — PDF pp. 6443–6448, 6468–6471

> Extractor note (not source text): Excerpts (cover at PDF p. 6439) on cost allocation and revenue allocation among RS/RH/RA and the commercial classes.

<!-- PDF page 6443 (printed p. 4) -->

The GMH class comprises customers whose “sole method of space heating” is electricity, except that customers may obtain supplemental heat from renewable sources. The Rate GMH customer count is much smaller than the corresponding Rate GM counts, by a factor of 8 to 10. The tariff is designed to be more attractive to heating loads, with the demand charge applying only in the summer months, and with a higher energy charge in the winter months. For cost allocation purposes, the Company splits the GMH class into GMH<25 kW and GMH>=25 kW, much like the GM subclasses, although the tariff charges are not differentiated. On a per-customer basis, average electricity usage for this class and the two sub-classes are moderately lower than those for Rate GM.

The 759 Rate GL customers are general service customers with minimum demand of 300 kW.[^knd2] Average per-customer use is more than ten times that for GM>=25, at about 3,400 MWh per year. The base rate tariff consists of a hybrid fixed/demand charge based on the 300 kW of minimum demand, and a demand charge for peak demand above 300 kW.

Like Rate GMH, Rate GLH is a class limited to customers using only electricity for heat, with demand charges applying only in the summer. Average customer size is similar to that for Rate GL. There are only about 90 customers in this rate class.

The Rate GL and GLH classes apply to customers up to 5,000 kW in contract demand, at which point Rate L applies.

**Q.** Why does the Company have a separate “heating” class for GM and GL customers?

**A.** The Company has both residential and non-residential rate classes that are targeted at electric heat customers.

The Residential Service Heating (RH) class is open to residential customers who use electricity as the “sole primary method of space heating.” RH rates are the same as regular residential service (RS), except that the energy charge is discounted in the winter season.[^knd3]

[^knd2]: The tariff does not appear to indicate how the 300 kW is determined, but since the minimum tariff charge is based on 300 kW of demand, it does not really matter.

[^knd3]: The Company also offers a Rate RA, for Residential add-on heat pump service. Rate design for this class is conceptually similar to that for Rate RH, although Rate RA cost to serve is modestly lower than that for Rate RS.

<!-- PDF page 6444 (printed p. 5) -->

As such, any residential customer would pay a lower average rate under Rate RH than Rate RS. This tariff design for distribution services does not reflect the Company’s allocated costs, which show that the cost of serving RH customers exceeds that of serving RS customers.[^knd4] This result obtains because the Company, like many EDCs, uses a “non-coincident peak” (“NCP”) demand allocator for each rate class, which then reflects the winter peak for the RH class in its allocated cost of service study (“ACOSS”). In all likelihood, the discounted rate for the RH class is something of an anachronism from the time of bundled rates, when DLC was attempting to encourage customers to switch to electric heat.[^knd5]

The GMH and GLH classes appear to fit the same model, namely that they are anachronistic rate classes that were designed to provide preferential rates to electric heat customers under bundled rates. Like Rate RH, the Company’s cost of service analysis indicates that the cost to serve the GMH and GLH customers is higher on a per kWh basis than for the comparable GM and GL rate classes.[^knd6]

There are two generic reasons why the Company may want to retain separate “heating” rate classes:

- First, the Company may believe that the costing method used in its ACOSS is not consistent with reality, and that the diversity of demands provided by electric heating customers serves to reduce the overall per-unit cost of distribution equipment. Thus, for example, if the same electric distribution equipment is serving both RS and RH customers, the cost to provide service to heating customers may not actually be higher than that to serve non-heating customers.

[^knd4]: As shown in RDK WP1, the demand-related cost per kWh is higher for Rate RH than for Rate RS, and the customer-related cost per customer is higher for Rate RH than for Rate RS.

[^knd5]: As shown in RDK WP1, the _coincident peak_ load factor for Rate RH is much higher than that for Rate RS, and thus costs related to _coincident peak_ (CP) demands would be lower on a per-unit basis for Rate RH. In the days before rate unbundling, costs allocated on a CP basis were typically generation and transmission costs, which are not the subject of this proceeding.

[^knd6]: RDK WP1 .

<!-- PDF page 6445 (printed p. 6) -->

- Second, the Company may believe that the cost to serve heating customers is indeed materially higher than that to serve non-heating customers, and it wishes to retain the heating classes to provide preferential rates to non-heating customers, and to target rates for heating customers at the behavior which causes the higher costs, namely the winter peak demands.

In my testimony in the Company’s last base rates case, I raised the concerns that the rate design for the GMH and GLH rate classes did not appear to be consistent with the costing philosophy in the Company’s ACOSS. My concerns were addressed, at least in part, by the following provisions in the settlement of that matter:

_“70. The Company agrees to undertake an evaluation of the GMH and GLH rate classes as_ _described in DLC Statement No. 16-R at page 18. The Company will provide the detailed_ _results of its evaluation and any resulting rate design proposals with its filing in its next base_ _rate proceeding.”_[^knd7]

The referenced testimony at Statement No. 16-R in that proceeding stated,

_“The Company would be amenable to undertaking an internal review process to evaluate_ _all or some of the following for potential consideration in a future rate case: 1) winter_ _demand charge for heating classes, 2) phase out and merge the heating classes into non-_ _heating classes, 3) closing the heating classes to new customers. The Company would take_ _into consideration customer bill impacts along with customer satisfaction.”_[^knd8]

To the best of my knowledge, the Company’s internal review process was undertaken by the Company’s independent expert, Mr. Gorman, and it is presented at pages 32-35 of DLC Statement No. 13. My understanding for Mr. Gorman’s key observations is as follows:

- Customer load profiles for GMH and GLH customers are substantially different from average load profiles for the corresponding GM and GL rate classes, primarily

[^knd7]: Docket No. R-2021-3024750, Recommended Decision, October 5, 2021 at 39.

[^knd8]: Docket No. R-2021-3024750, Duquesne Light Company Statement No. 16-R at 18.

<!-- PDF page 6446 (printed p. 7) -->

in that the heating classes have winter peaks while the non-heating classes have mostly summer peaks;

- There is less demand diversity within the heating classes than that within the non-heating classes (presumably reflecting the temperature sensitivity of heating customer peaks);

- Load factors for heating system customers are lower than that for non-heating customers;

- Combining the heating and non-heating rate classes would, all other factors being equal, reduce costs allocated to these classes, due to the benefits of load diversity that would be reflected in the Company’s cost allocation method;

- Citing to Mr. Ogden’s non-quantitative evidence in the last base rates case, Mr. Gorman concludes that a winter energy charge is a more appropriate rate design for the heating rate classes for both Company revenue and customer bill stability.

From this analysis, I conclude that the Company has determined that (a) the cost to serve heating classes is driven by winter peak demands, (b) heating classes are more costly to serve than non-heating classes and rates should (at least eventually) reflect that fact, and (c) the Company’s revenue stability is a more important criterion than cost causation for heating class rate design.

**Q.** Is this a reasonable approach for the Company?

**A.** The Company’s approach has advantages and disadvantages. The Company is certainly correct that the heating classes have load patterns that are very different from those of the non-heating classes. In addition, under the Company’s costing philosophy wherein distribution costs for the heating classes are related primarily to winter demands, retaining separate rate classes allows the Company to better target the tariff charges at the demands which cause the costs to be incurred.

The disadvantages begin with the accuracy of the costing method. While the Company’s approach to cost causation is consistent with industry practice, it is conceptually problematic.

<!-- PDF page 6447 (printed p. 8) -->

The Company’s “NCP” method assumes that distribution equipment is sized to meet the diversified demand of each individual class and thus reflects the benefits of load diversity within the class, but it does not reflect load diversity between rate classes. In effect, the method implicitly assumes that no components of the distribution system are used to serve both summer-peaking non-heat customers and winter-peaking heat customers. In practice, each component of the distribution system must be sized to meet the diversified peak demand of all customers downstream, which is likely to (a) reflect much less intra-class diversity than assumed by a classwide NCP demand, and (b) reflect some load diversity between rate classes, because the equipment is likely to serve multiple classes. By segregating the heating and non-heating classes, the NCP demand measure (for both residential and non-residential customers) may not reasonably represent cost causation.

Moreover, there are potential practical problems associated with the Company’s approach. These special heating classes were envisioned as a discounted rate to attract heating customers. Thus, customers were encouraged to participate, if they could demonstrate that their primary heating systems were electric. Under the Company’s current cost analysis, however, the heating customers are generally more expensive to serve, at least on an average per-kWh basis. Depending on the load shape of the individual customer, some heating service customers may experience lower bills under the non-heating tariff.

Thus, if the Company wants to charge electric heat customers for their contribution to winter peaks, rather than being able to rely on the customer to sign up for the discounted rate, it may become incumbent upon the utility to require that customers whose primary heating systems are electric are assigned to the heating rate classes. In effect, to ensure that the heating customers pay the higher costs they cause, the utility would need to establish measures to identify customers in non-heating classes who have electric heat or who have recently converted to electric heat, and to reassign those customers to the heating service rate classes. It is, of course, extremely unlikely that the Company would have any interest in this type of enforcement. I therefore conclude that the Company desires to retain both options to allow customers to choose whichever tariff option is the more economically attractive.

<!-- PDF page 6448 (printed p. 9) -->

Having considered the Company’s arguments in this respect, I do not disagree with the Company’s approach which appears to be that it will simply let the potential problems with the heating tariffs gradually solve themselves. If the heating classes continue to exhibit a higher cost of service (and rates are cost-based), some electric heat customers will transition to the non-heating rate classes to obtain the lower rates. This process would begin with the higher load factor electric heat customers, who are most likely to see lower rates under the regular tariffs. As those customers move out, unit costs for the heating classes will rise disproportionately, and the economic incentive to transition will increase.

To avoid artificially encouraging or discouraging the use of the electric heat rates, I recommend that the Company follow two standard ratemaking practices. First, rates for each class should be set as close to the cost of service as practicable. Second, customers should be educated and provided with tools to be able to evaluate which tariff option is most attractive for their individual loads.

| 3. | Cost Allocation |
| -- | --------------: |

**Q.** What is the purpose of a utility’s allocated cost of service study (“ACOSS”)?

**A.** The most important criterion for setting regulated utility rates is the cost incurred by the utility for providing the service.[^knd9] To assign costs to specific customers, utilities aggregate customers into rate classes, within which the customers have similar load sizes, seasonal consumption, peak demand patterns, and other characteristics. An ACOSS is an analytical tool with which the utility’s total cost (or “revenue requirement”) is allocated among each of the rate classes. These allocated costs are then used as a key input in determining the total revenues that the utility plans to recover from each rate class through tariff rates.

In using the results from an ACOSS to develop class revenue requirements, utilities and regulatory authorities usually have a longer-term goal of moving the revenue recovered from each class as close as possible to the costs allocated to that class. Thus, rate classes whose revenues substantially exceed allocated costs are assigned either relatively low rate increases

[^knd9]: The Commonwealth Court affirmed this basic principle, referring to cost of service as the “polestar” criterion. Lloyd v. Pennsylvania Public Utility Commission, 904 A.2d 1010, 1020 (Pa. Cmwlth. 2006).

<!-- PDF page 6468 (printed p. 29) -->

### 4. Revenue Allocation

**Q.** What is revenue allocation?

**A.** Revenue allocation is the assignment of the dollar net increase or decrease to each of the Company’s rate classes in a base rates proceeding. In contrast, _rate design_ determines how the allocated revenue is recovered from individual ratepayers within each class. From a cost recovery standpoint, revenue allocation addresses _inter-class_ cross-subsidization issues, while rate design addresses _intra-class_ cross-subsidization issues.

**Q.** What are the primary economic and regulatory criteria for revenue allocation?

<!-- PDF page 6469 (printed p. 30) -->

**A.** In general, allocated cost is the primary criterion used by regulators in the revenue allocation process. Most utilities and regulators adopt a policy in a base rates proceeding of attempting to move revenues more into line with allocated costs by varying the magnitude of the rate increases for the individual classes. However, regulators also subject the rate increases to other non-cost criteria of ratemaking. Of the traditional rate design criteria, the most common non-cost considerations in the revenue allocation process are:

- the _gradualism_ principle (or avoidance of “rate shock”), in which large rate increases for individual customers or classes of customers are avoided; and

- the _value of service_ principle, which is often used to mitigate rate increases for customers or customer classes with relatively price-elastic demand.[^knd29]

Using these criteria, the utility will develop a proposal for assigning the increase in the revenue requirement among the classes that reflects both cost and non-cost considerations. With this proposal, the ACOSS can be simulated at both present and proposed rates to evaluate the magnitude of “progress” has been made toward the policy of achieving cost-based rates.

**Q.** What is the Commission’s standard for measuring progress toward cost-based rates?

**A.** For many years, participants in Pennsylvania utility regulatory proceedings have relied on a metric known as the “indexed rate of return,” or “relative rate of return.” DLC Witness Ogden confirms that the Company relies on this flawed metric.

The conceptual and practical problems associated with this metric are reviewed in detail in Appendix A.

Based on the analysis in Appendix A, for this proceeding, I relied on the revenue-cost (“R-C”) ratio metric. The “R-C” metric represents (unsurprisingly) the ratio of class revenues to

[^knd29]: See, for example, Principles of Public Utility Rates, Second Edition, Bonbright, Danielsen, Kamerschen, 1988, pages 383 to 387. Note that the criteria in this text apply to the overall development of a utility rate structure. The criteria that I discuss in this testimony are those that apply to the revenue allocation portion of the process, which is only one aspect of the overall development of utility rates.

<!-- PDF page 6470 (printed p. 31) -->

class allocated costs, and thus implicitly recognizes the subsidy as a percentage of the class revenue requirement.

**Q.** Is the Company’s proposed revenue allocation consistent with its own ACOSS?

**A.** The Company’s revenue allocation proposal is somewhat consistent with its ACOSS results, although it is relatively cautious in terms of moving rates into line with allocated costs. As shown in RDK WP1, a more cost-based revenue allocation would generally assign a lower rate increase to the RS, GS, GMH<25 and lighting classes, and higher increases to the RH, GM>=25, GMH>=25, GL and L classes.

However, because of my disagreements with the Company’s ACOSS method, I developed an alternative revenue allocation based on the cost allocation results detailed in RDK WP3.

**Q.** Does your alternative cost allocation analysis imply an alternative revenue allocation?

**A.** Yes. To develop my alternative revenue allocation proposal, I began by calculating the rate change needed to bring proposed revenues into line with allocated cost using my alternative ACOSS. I then made two adjustments to those values. First, to reflect concerns regarding rate gradualism, I limited the maximum increase to 1.5 times system average. DLC’s proposed system average is 15.9 percent, and thus the maximum increase is set at 23.8 percent. Based on my ACOSS model, this constraint applies to all of the residential classes, the GS class and the UMS class. Second, I set the minimum rate change at zero, to avoid rate reductions. This constraint applied to the GM>=25, GMH>=25, GL, L, HVPS, and SE classes.

To a large extent, the aggregate effect of these two changes results in a relatively small need for reallocation. The net effect of these two adjustments is a revenue shortfall of $5.3 million. I reallocated that shortfall to those classes that are not capped by the 1.5X rule, based on current rate revenues. In this way, the unconstrained classes all contribute to the net subsidy needed for the constrained classes. Because most class increases are limited by one of the two constraints, the reallocated amounts are assigned only to a few rate classes (GM<25, GMH<25, GLH and SL). The details for these calculations are provided in RDK WP3 “Summary” worksheet. My alternative revenue allocation is shown in Table RDK-5 below, compared to the Company’s filed proposal.

As shown in Table RDK-S2, the Company’s proposal will actually result in revenues moving farther away from allocated costs for several relatively large rate classes, notably the regular residential class (RS), the GM and GMH classes over 25 kW, the GL class and the Rate L class. Note that in addition to failing the revenue-cost ratio tests, the Company’s revenue allocation fails the basic common-sense test, namely that it proposes a below-average system increase to an under-recovering class (RS), and above-average increases to over-recovering rate classes (GM>25, GMH>25, GL and L). As such, the Company’s proposal fails to

<!-- PDF page 6528 (printed p. 6) -->

comply with the “polestar” criterion for revenue allocation in Pennsylvania, namely allocated cost.

**Q.** If the Commission adopts the Company’s rebuttal ACOSS methodology, what revenue allocation do you propose?

**A.** I developed a revenue allocation proposal based on the Company’s rebuttal ACOSS in RDK WPS1, summarized below. In so doing, I followed the methodology that I laid out in my direct testimony. That approach was:

- Derive the rate change needed to move class revenues into line with allocated costs;

- Adjust those increases to eliminate any rate reductions and set the increase for those classes to zero;

- Adjust any increases to no more than 1.5 times system average, to reflect rate gradualism;

- Reallocate the adjustments from the prior two steps to the rest of the rate classes;

- Retest the 1.5 times system limit and adjust as necessary.

Based on this approach, if the Company’s rebuttal ACOSS is adopted by the Commission, I recommend that the revenue allocation shown in Table RDK-S3 below be adopted. As shown, this proposed revenue allocation has the effect of moving most rate classes quite close to allocated costs. Note also that the modest over-recovery from the RS class is modestly below the subsidies remaining for the RH and RA residential classes.

## Geller direct (CAUSE-PA Statement No. 1) — PDF pp. 6571–6572, 6574, 6578

> Extractor note (not source text): Excerpts on the residential fixed charge (Rates RS/RH/RA) and CAP. Table 7 and Table 8 were rebuilt by hand and checked against page images.

### IV. RATE DESIGN

**Q:** Please briefly describe DLC’s residential fixed customer charge proposal.

**A:** In its initial filings, DLC proposes to continue its structure of using a combination of fixed charge and energy-based (i.e. usage) rates for all residential rate classes – Residential Service Rate RS, Residential Heating Service Rate RH, and Residential Service Add-On Heat Pump Rate RA. Table 7 describes the current and proposed customer charge and energy charge for Rate RS, RH, and RA:

**Table 7: Current v. Proposed Fixed Customer Charge and Volumetric Charge (RS; RH; RA)**[^gld68]

|                                        | RS Current | RS Proposed |      RH Current |     RH Proposed |      RA Current |     RA Proposed |
| -------------------------------------- | ---------: | ----------: | --------------: | --------------: | --------------: | --------------: |
| Fixed Customer Charge                  |     $12.50 |      $15.00 |          $12.50 |          $15.00 |          $12.50 |          $15.00 |
| Volumetric Charge (cents per kWh hour) |     7.0993 |      8.4662 | 6.0206 (Winter) | 7.5466 (Winter) | 2.4580 (Winter) | 3.5598 (Winter) |
|                                        |            |             | 7.0993 (Summer) | 8.4662 (Summer) | 7.0993 (Summer) | 8.4662 (Summer) |

As the Table shows, DLC is proposing a 20% increase to the fixed portion of its residential rates, from $12.50 to $15.00.

**Q:** How will DLC’s proposal to increase the fixed portion of its rates by 20% impact its low income customers?

**A:** DLC’s fixed charge proposal will undermine the ability of consumers to control costs through energy efficiency, conservation, and consumption reduction, which is particularly problematic for low income consumers. Due to disparities in housing and heating system

[^gld68]: DLC Exhibit DBO-2, Redline, Supplement No. 71 to Electric – Pa. PUC No. 25, Third Revised Page Nos. 38, 40, 43.

<!-- PDF page 6572 (printed p. 32) -->

efficiency, low income households often have greater potential to reduce energy and gain cost benefits through installation of comprehensive efficiency and conservation measures. Low income customers already struggle profoundly to pay for electric service and rely on the ability to offset high bills through conservation and usage reduction. Regardless of the level of household usage, any increase to the fixed charge erodes the ability of consumers to effectively deploy efficiency and conservation measures to achieve bill savings.

**Q:** Would DLC’s proposed increase to the fixed charge impact the effectiveness of its Low Income Usage Reduction Program (LIURP)?

**A:** Yes. DLC’s proposal to dramatically increase its fixed charge will undermine the regulatory goals for LIURP. The Commission’s LIURP regulations explicitly provide that LIURP is intended to help low income customers to reduce their bills and, in turn, to “decrease the incidence and risk of customer payment delinquencies and the attendant utility costs associated with uncollectible accounts expense, collection costs and arrearage carrying costs.”[^gld69] As discussed in detail below, LIURP – when adequately available and structured – is effective at achieving these goals, and provides meaningful usage reduction to program participants. The ability to save money through energy efficiency is tied directly to a bill structure that bases costs on throughput. But if more residential customer costs are shifted to the fixed charge, the achievable bill savings – and the corresponding impact on bill payment behavior – will erode. Thus, increasing the fixed customer charge limits the ability of the LIURP program to help program participants to achieve meaningful bill savings.

[^gld69]: 52 Pa. Code § 58.1 (“The programs are intended to assist low-income customers conserve energy and reduce residential energy bills. The reduction in energy bills should decrease the incidence and risk of customer payment delinquencies and the attendant utility costs associated with uncollectible accounts expense, collection costs and arrearage carrying costs.”).

<!-- PDF page 6574 (printed p. 34) -->

### A. Customer Assistance Program

**Q:** Please describe the Company’s Customer Assistance Program (CAP).

**A:** DLC’s CAP is available to customers with gross household income at or below 150% FPL.[^gld71] As explained above, DLC implemented a PIP CAP design in January 2021.[^gld72] Under the PIP CAP design, participants are billed one of the following CAP rates: (1) a percentage of their monthly gross household income; (2) the average monthly bill; or (3) the customer’s actual usage, if it is less than a PIPP and average monthly bill.[^gld73] Customers who report $0 income are required to make the minimum CAP payment of $20 for residential (non-heat) service customers and $40 for residential heating customers (except where a customer’s actual usage in a given month results in a bill less than the minimum payment in which case, the customer is billed based on actual usage).[^gld74] While DLC’s current USECP is silent regarding the frequency of its CAP rate review, DLC indicated in its last USECP proceeding that it reviews and adjusts CAP rates every four months (three times per year).[^gld75] The following maximum CAP credits are applied on an annual basis:

**Table 8: DLC CAP Maximum Annual CAP Credit Limits**[^gld76]

| Effective 1/15/22 | Nonheating Customers | Electric Heat Customers |
| ----------------- | -------------------: | ----------------------: |
| 0-50% FPL         |               $1,700 |                  $2,500 |
| 51-100% FPL       |               $1,500 |                  $2,000 |
| 101-150% FPL      |               $1,000 |                  $1,400 |

[^gld71]: Id. at 7.

[^gld72]: See Duquesne Light Company Universal Service and Energy Conservation Plan, Order on Reconsideration, at Appendix A (entered April 19, 2018, at Docket No. M-2016-2543423).

[^gld73]: 2020-2025 DLC USECP at 7-8.

[^gld74]: 2020-2025 DLC USECP at 8.

[^gld75]: DLC USECP for 2020-2025 Submitted in Compliance with 52 Pa. Code § 54.74, Order, Docket No. M-2019-3008227, at 47-48 (order entered April 21, 2022).

[^gld76]: 2020-2025 DLC USECP at 9.

<!-- PDF page 6578 (printed p. 38) -->

I am also concerned that DLC does not appear to treat its de facto heating customers as electric heating customers. Despite the fact de facto heating customers are reliant on electricity to heat their home, DLC applies the non-heating maximum CAP credit limit to these households. When a family is unable to use a primary heating system – or their heating source is inadequate to keep their home at a safe temperature – they often resort to dangerous, high usage / high-cost heating methods including electric space-heaters, electric stoves, and/or portable generators. Reliance on these alternative sources for heat increases the risk of carbon monoxide poisoning and house fires.[^gld84] In addition to safety concerns, reliance on de facto heating sources can result in substantially higher direct and indirect costs to households, depletion of CAP credits, acute and chronic rate unaffordability, accrual of exorbitant arrears, and involuntary termination. DLC should ensure that families reliant on de facto heating are receiving adequate CAP credits based on their status as a heating customer, and should in turn assist the household to resolve issues with their primary heating system to reduce reliance on unsafe alternative heating sources.

**Q:** What are the consequences for CAP customers who exceed their maximum CAP credit limits?

**A:** The consequences of exceeding the maximum CAP credit limit are severe. The monthly bill of customers who exceed the maximum CAP credit limit is substantially higher – adding, on average, well over $100 to the monthly CAP bill.[^gld85] Table 10 below shows the average increase to CAP customers’ bills in January each year (2022 through 2024) after they exceeded their maximum CAP credit limits:

[^gld84]: Richard Campbell, Home Heating Fires, National Fire Protection Association (NFPA), (Jan. 2021), available at: https://www.nfpa.org/News-and-Research/Data-research-and-tools/US-Fire-Problem/Heating-equipment

[^gld85]: CAUSE-PA III-7, Attachment.

start of 2023 (including pending jobs).[^gls19] It is imperative that these customers are accounted for in the policies and procedures related to DLC’s CAP. While DLC tracks the number of de facto heating jobs performed, DLC does not maintain a unique classification for de facto heating customers.[^gls20] It is therefore unclear how many DLC customers actually rely on de facto heating. Without this tracking it is impossible to determine how many de facto heating customers, including de facto heating CAP customers, DLC is serving. I am similarly concerned that DLC’s definition of de facto heating customers is unnecessarily restrictive. Through additional discovery, DLC indicates that de facto heating customers are classified as such only if they (1) have a delivered fuel source and indicate they cannot afford to fill their fuel tank, and are using electric space heating; (2) had a gas furnace but no longer have gas service due to a line leak that they cannot afford to pay, and the gas company cannot restore service, leading the customer to heat their homes entirely with electric space heaters; or (3) have an old, inefficient, or nonworking electric furnace and are using space heat.[^gls21] DLC’s definition of de facto heating does not include all instances where a customer is reliant on an alternative heating source as a primary or supplementary heating source when their primary or central heating system is inadequate, nonfunctional, or public utility service has been terminated. DLC’s restrictive definition of de facto heating limits the identification of customers who are relying on unsafe and inefficient alternative heating sources, and in turn the provision of services to these customers to remediate unsafe and unnecessarily costly conditions and to economically assist. For these reasons, I stand firmly by my recommendation that DLC be required to classify de facto heating customers as electric heating customers for the purpose of determining applicable

CAP credit limits. In addition, I recommend that DLC be required to identify and track de facto heating customers. I also recommend that DLC be required to revise its definition of de facto heating customers, so that all customers reliant on an alternative electric-based heating source for primary or supplemental heat are classified as electric heating customers for the purposes of CAP. I similarly recommend that DLC be required to integrate this revised definition into its most-current USECP so that customers with de facto heating can be identified and receive the appropriate maximum CAP level, LIURP and other energy efficiency and repair/remediation services.

**Q:** Please summarize Mr. Schuck’s rebuttal testimony in response to your recommendation to assist customers who reach and/or exceed their CAP maximum credit limits.

**A:** In my direct testimony, I explained that DLC’s maximum annual CAP credit limits are already grossly inadequate to meet the need for assistance, even at existing rates.[^gls22] More than one in every four (28%) of CAP participants exceeded their maximum CAP credit limits in 2023 – and thousands of CAP customers were thereby charged categorically unaffordable rates.[^gls23] To address this unacceptable outcome, I recommended that the Company implement a system under which customers that reach their CAP limit and do not qualify for an exemption are first prioritized for LIURP or other energy efficiency programs to address high usage and are only removed from CAP if the customer refuses LIURP after being determined eligible.[^gls24] Mr. Schuck opposes this recommendation to more closely coordinate DLC’s CAP and LIURP.[^gls25] He acknowledges that both programs are intended to work in concert to provide assistance, but nevertheless asserts, in

regulation and Commission orders.[^gls69] Moreover, in its recent decision in the PGW rate proceeding, the Commission found that tabling consideration related to universal service issues in the context of PGW’s next USECP proceeding would result in denying low income customers relief for an extended period of time.[^gls70] The Commission found that such results would unreasonably “thwart the purpose of universal service, which is to help low-income customers maintain their natural gas services. See, 66 Pa. C.S. § 2202.” The Commission also found persuasive arguments that base rate proceedings provide a more formal review process for USECP review processes.[^gls71] In sum, it is neither just nor reasonable, and squarely contrary to the public interest, to defer consideration of whether low income customers can reasonably afford to access and maintain services in their homes – and whether amendment to low income programming, and to policies and procedures related to these programs is needed – to a proceeding at a later date. To raise basic energy rates, as DLC is proposing to do, without first ensuring that low income customers are able to receive affordable service is neither just nor reasonable, and is squarely contrary to the public interest. Indeed, universal accessibility is a polestar principle of ratemaking for essential utility services. III. RESPONSE TO DLC WITNESS DAVID B. OGDEN (DLC ST. 14-R)

**Q:** Please summarize the rebuttal testimony of DLC expert witness David B. Ogden (DLC St. 14-R) to which you wish to respond.

**A:** In my direct testimony, I recommend that DLC’s proposal to increase the residential customer charge from $12.50 per month to $15.00 per month should be rejected, as the increase in the fixed charge will undermine the ability of DLC customers to control costs through energy

efficiency, conservation, and consumption.[^gls72] Mr. Ogden argues that, on average, DLC’s low income customers would pay more if the current customer charge remained at $12.50 and the increase was applied solely to the volumetric charge. Mr. Ogden argues that this is true for both low income customers not enrolled in CAP – as well as CAP enrollees who may exhaust their maximum annual discount sooner if the fixed charge portion were not increased.[^gls73] In response to OCA’s proposal, which recommends that DLC maintain its fixed charge at $12.50, Mr. Ogden argues that the net monthly benefit to CAP customers utilizing a period of 12 months ended in May 2024 would be $0.27, and the net monthly benefit for confirmed low income non-CAP customers would be $0.10.[^gls74]

**Q:** What is your response to Mr. Ogden’s testimony that increasing the fixed customer charge to $15.00 would benefit low income customers.

**A:** First, I question the assumptions of Mr. Ogden’s calculations that conclude that increasing the fixed charge to $15.00 would benefit low income customers, compared to maintaining the fixed charge portion of residential customers bills and applying any increase to the volumetric portion. Mr. Ogden’s calculations appear to be based on average usage levels for CAP and non-CAP customers, and therefore do not account for variations in usage level which may occur through adoption of energy efficiency measures through assistance programs. Indeed, as the below chart shows, the _median_ (middle) usage of CAP customers and confirmed low income customers, between January 2022 and December 2023, consistently tracks lower than the _mean_ (average) usage of these same customer segments:[^gls75]

Median Billed Usage (Jan. 2022 - Dec. 2023)” and “Chart 2: CAP Customers, Mean v. Median Billed Usage (Jan. 2022 - Dec. 2023)”, each plotting monthly Mean_Billed_kWh and Median_Billed_kWh (about 400–1,000 kWh). The charts are not reproduced and no values were read off them.

The lower median billed usage of CAP participants and confirmed low income customers compared to mean usage levels speaks to certain higher usage customers in these segments which cause an increase in reported mean usage levels. These differences further illustrate that Mr. Ogden’s calculations of bill impacts for maintaining DLC’s fixed customer charges do not account for variations in customer usage – and underscores the need to target low income high usage customers for comprehensive conservation and efficiency services.

<!-- PDF page 6842 (printed p. 32) -->

Moreover, even if one were to set aside the flaws in Mr. Ogden’s analysis, his calculations represent minor average savings for CAP and non-CAP low income customers. For example, Mr. Ogden argues that the net monthly benefit to confirmed low income (non-CAP) customers for a period of 12 months ended in May 2024 would be $0.10, pursuant to DLC’s proposal to increase the fixed charge to $15.00.[^gls76] By comparison, the percent energy savings from treated LIURP household for electric baseload and electric heat between 2019-2022 ranged between 5-8%.[^gls77] Applying this average rate of energy savings, a confirmed low income customer using 700kWh/month would save, on average, between 35-56 kWh/month. At current distribution rates, before any rate increase is approved, a low income customer at this usage level would save between $2.48 and $3.97 each month on their volumetric distribution rate by participating in LIURP[^gls78] – dwarfing the $0.10/month savings touted by Mr. Ogden by increasing the fixed charge. Increasing the fixed charge will erode the ability of low income customers to achieve savings on their monthly energy bills through careful conservation and adoption of efficiency measures, including through LIURP. Such a result stands in stark contrast to the well-established policy of the Commonwealth to reduce energy consumption and, in turn, reduce ever-increasing strain on the grid.

Duquesne Light Company Docket No. R-2024-3046523 Interrogatories of Coalition for Affordable Utility Services and Energy Efficiency in Pennsylvania (CAUSE-PA)

Set VII Witness: Edwin Schuck

### CAUSE-PA-VII-6

6. Please answer: a. Under what circumstances does DLC currently classify de facto heating customers as heating customers for the purposes of assignment of rate classes? Please explain. b. Under what circumstances does DLC currently classify de facto heating customers as heating customers for the purposes of its universal service and energy conservation programs? Please explain.

### Response:

a. Duquesne Light Company currently does not have a unique classification in its customer information system for de facto heat customers. b. For the Duquesne Light Income Eligible energy efficiency programs customers are classified as de facto heat if they,

- have a delivered fuel source (Propane, etc.) and indicate that they are not cannot afford to fill their fuel tank, and are using electric space heat

- had a gas furnace but they no longer have gas service to the home due to a line leak that the customer could not pay to repair, and the gas company is not able to restore service to the home, therefore the customer is heating the home entirely with electric space heaters.

- Have old inefficient or non-working electric furnace and using space heat to heat the home.

The use of electric space heat is verified through the customers winter usage. Non-electric heat customers with over 1,000 kWh used in one winter month are suspected of using de facto. In addition, customers are also identified during the in-home LIURP visit. Once a

<!-- PDF page 6877 -->

customer is provided service the Company changes their rate class from residential service (RS) to residential add on heat pump (RA) rate.

## Direct Testimony of Lisa V. Perry (Walmart Inc.), Revenue Allocation — PDF pp. 6903–6905

> Extractor note (not source text): Excerpt on relative rates of return (RROR). Printed testimony page numbers are 14–16. Tables 2 and 3 were rebuilt from the page text and checked against the PDF.

<!-- PDF page 6903 -->

#### _(B) Revenue Allocation_

**Q.** HOW DOES THE COMPANY REPRESENT IF RATES FOR A CUSTOMER CLASS ACCURATELY REFLECT THE UNDERLYING COST CAUSATION?

**A.** The Company reflects this relationship in their cost-of-service results through the use of class-specific rates of return. These rates of return can be converted into a "relative rate of return" or "RROR," which is an indexed measure of the relationship of the rate of return for an individual customer class to the total system rate of return. A RROR greater than 1.0 means that the customer class is paying rates in excess of costs incurred by the Company to serve that class. Conversely, a RROR less than 1.0 means that the customer class is paying rates less than the costs incurred by the Company to serve that class. As such, those rate classes with a RROR greater than 1.0 are subsidizing the classes with a RROR less than 1.0.

**Q.** HAS THE COMPANY CALCULATED CLASS RRORs BASED ON ITS PROPOSED COST OF SERVICE RESULTS?

**A.** Yes, as shown in Table 2 below.

<!-- PDF page 6904 -->

**Table 2. Class Rates of Return and RRORs, Current Rates, Duquesne Proposed Allocated Cost of Service Results.**

| Customer Class | Rate of Return |   RROR |
| -------------- | -------------: | -----: |
| RS             |          6.95% |   1.16 |
| RH             |          4.96% |   0.83 |
| RA             |          5.37% |   0.90 |
| GS             |          6.71% |   1.12 |
| GM<25          |          6.30% |   1.05 |
| GM>25          |          5.39% |   0.90 |
| GMH<25         |          6.30% |   1.05 |
| GMH>25         |          4.89% |   0.82 |
| GL             |          4.93% |   0.82 |
| GLH            |          2.65% |   0.44 |
| L              |          4.36% |   0.73 |
| HVPS           |           874% | 146.17 |
| SE             |          8.47% |   1.42 |
| SL             |          8.08% |   1.35 |
| UMS            |          3.44% |   0.58 |
| System Total   |          5.98% |    1.0 |

Source: Ex. 6-10, p. 1, line 4; p. 2, line 4.

**Q.** WHAT IS YOUR UNDERSTANDING OF THE COMPANY'S REVENUE ALLOCATION PROPOSAL?

**A.** I understand that the Company's proposal to allocate the rate increase among customer classes follows a structured approach. First, the Company continues its longstanding objectives, established since its 2006 distribution rate case, of aligning customer classes closer to their cost of service levels or achieving a 1.0 RROR.[^per13] The Company then mitigates the impact on customers by capping the increase for any class at 1.5 times the overall system average increase.[^per14] Finally, the Company

[^per13]: _See_ Duquesne Statement No. 14, Direct Testimony of David B. Ogden ("Ogden Direct"), p. 7, lines 4-7.

[^per14]: _See id._ at 7, lines 14-18.

<!-- PDF page 6905 -->

addresses and reduces class subsidies at both current and proposed rates.[^per15] According to the Company's proposed revenue allocation, the RROR for each class would be as follows:

**Table 3. Class RRORs, Current Rates v. Duquesne Proposed.**

| Customer Class | Current RROR | Proposed RROR |
| -------------- | -----------: | ------------: |
| RS             |         1.16 |          1.11 |
| RH             |         0.83 |          0.91 |
| RA             |         0.90 |          0.98 |
| GS             |         1.12 |          1.08 |
| GM<25          |         1.05 |          1.04 |
| GM>25          |         0.90 |          0.93 |
| GMH<25         |         1.05 |          1.03 |
| GMH>25         |         0.82 |          0.87 |
| GL             |         0.82 |          0.87 |
| GLH            |         0.44 |          0.60 |
| L              |         0.73 |          0.81 |
| HVPS           |       146.17 |         99.15 |
| SE             |         1.42 |          1.22 |
| SL             |         1.35 |          1.19 |
| UMS            |         0.58 |          0.75 |
| System Total   |          1.0 |           1.0 |

Source: Ex. 6-10, p. 1, lines 4 and 34; p. 2, lines 4 and 34.

**Q.** DOES THE COMPANY'S PROPOSED REVENUE ALLOCATION BRING CUSTOMER CLASSES TO THEIR COST BASED LEVELS?

**A.** No, it does not. As shown in Table 3 above, although the Company's proposed revenue allocation brings each class closer to its cost of service level, all classes remain in either a subsidized or subsidizing position.

[^per15]: _See id._ at 7, lines 20-23.
